归纳推理任务中Transformer的不变学习动态

📅 2026-07-13 📄 arxiv 🔗 原文链接

原标题：Invariant Learning Dynamics of Transformers in Inductive Reasoning Tasks

中文摘要： 本文提出一个理论框架，解释Transformer模型中归纳推理能力的涌现。研究定义了一类统一的归纳任务，并理论证明模型训练动力学可被限制在高度可解释的低维“归纳推理不变流形”（IMIR）上。基于此框架，作者揭示了数据统计如何影响上下文学习与权重内学习的竞争，以及随机初始化如何决定“获胜”电路。此外，该框架还能自动检测模型中已学习的归纳电路，为理解大语言模型的学习动态提供了新视角。

Invariant Learning Dynamics of Transformers in Inductive Reasoning Tasks Tiberiu Musat ETH Zurich &Tiago Pimentel ETH Zurich &Nicolas Zucchet Stanford University &Thomas Hofmann ETH Zurich Correspondence at tiberiu@musat.ai , tiago.pimentel@inf.ethz.ch Abstract We present a theoretical framework to explain the emergence of inductive reasoning abilities in Transformer language models. While previous works on Transformer learning dynamics have so far been mostly tied to specific tasks, we study a generalized class of inductive tasks that unifies several synthetic tasks known in the literature, including in-context n n -grams and multi-hop reasoning. In this class, we theoretically prove that the training dynamics of attention models can be confined to a highly interpretable, low-dimensional invariant manifold. On this manifold, the learning dynamics are captured by a handful of interpretable coordinates rather than millions of parameters, making both theoretical and empirical analysis more tractable. Using this framework, we characterize how data statistics govern the competition between in-context and in-weights learning, we study how random initializations determine the ‘winning’ circuit when multiple solutions are possible, and we demonstrate that the coordinate frame associated with the manifold can be used to automatically detect which circuits have been learned in trained models. By casting circuit formation as a low-dimensional dynamical phenomenon, we take a step toward

关于 Transformer 如何学习的预测性理论。 图 1： 归纳

ontext n n -grams [ 7 , 8 , 9 ] or k k -hop induction [ 10 , 11 , 12 , 13 ] . Without a unifying theoretical framework, our understanding progresses slowly, and three important phenomena remain particularly puzzling. 1 Circuit competition is a fundamental aspect of language model training [ 14 , 15 , 16 ] . However, most previous theoretical works assume staged learning algorithms where only one component is trained at a time, while others are kept frozen [ 17 , 18 ] . A few works study the dynamical interaction of different components during training, but are limited to small transformers with only one [ 19 ] or two [ 20 ] attention heads. 2 Data distributional properties strongly influence the emergence of in-context learning [ 21 , 22 , 23 ] , but a mechanistic understanding is currently limited to phenomenological models [ 24 ] . 3 Randomly initialized networks contain high-performing subnetworks that can be trained in isolation, according to the lottery ticket hypothesis [ 25 ] , but we currently lack a mechanistic understanding of why specific initializations yield ‘winning’ tickets. Our paper’s central contribution is the Invariant Manifold of Inductive Reasoning (IMIR) : a low-dimensional subspace of the parameter space which exhibits self-contained learning dynamics. A model whose parameters belong to the IMIR will remain on the IMIR when updated via gradient descent, a behavior known in dynamical systems theory as an invariant manifold . We theoretically prove the e

xistence of the IMIR and provide its mathematical description ( section ˜ 3 ) for a generalized class of induction tasks ( section ˜ 2 ). Importantly, our framework considers all parameter interactions in a model during training, making it more tractable to study phenomena like circuit competition. Moreover, each dimension of the IMIR is highly interpretable, corresponding to a specific elementary action within a specific transformer component. This allows us to find reduced and interpretable descriptions of the transformer circuits responsible for inductive reasoning abilities. In section ˜ 4 , we leverage our framework to conduct a circuit competition study (addressing 1 ). Concretely, we first investigate the circuit competition between in-context learning and in-weights learning ( section ˜ 4.2 ), where we theoretically prove the data dependency of in-context learning (addressing 2 ). Second, we investigate the impact of random initialization on the competition between several in-context learning circuits within a single transformer model; we find that the winning circuit is determined by the model’s initialization, with sharp phase transitions between the regions of initialization space favoring each circuit (partially addressing 3 in this simplified setup). In section ˜ 5 , we show how the highly interpretable structure of the IMIR can be used for automatic detection of inductive circuits. Finally, in section ˜ 6 , we discuss other possible applications of our framework

Notably, this is one of the most studied inductive tasks. k k -hop induction [ 10 , 11 , 12 , 13 ] . This task generalizes the in-context associative recall task by considering chained pairs of associations. It has a similar structure to the previous task; however, once 𝐱 1 i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}^{i}_{1} is identified in the context, we may use 𝐱 2 i {{\color[rg

Several other tasks also fit this general

block-list structure, among them: indirect object identification [ 3 ] , conditional retrieval [ 11 ] , inverting permutations [ 27 ] , the in-context recall tasks used to study the emergence of sparse attention [ 19 ] , in-context language learning [ 7 ] , and reasoning with fragments of natural language [ 28 ] . We present these tasks in fig. ˜ 2 . Figure 2: We define a block-list class of language tasks where the next token must be predicted based on a list of token tuples. We then study the training dynamics in this class of tasks. 2.2 Data Symmetries In inductive reasoning tasks, predictions typically depend on high-level structure, rather than on arbitrary choices of tokens or absolute positions. We now describe a few symmetries present in our data, which may impose such structure. Token Symmetries. What a model must learn in an inductive task is the relation between tokens, not the identity of any particular token. For instance, in the associative recall task of section ˜ 2.1 , the rule “return the token that previously followed the query” is unaffected if we consistently relabel the vocabulary: the instance a ​ b ​ … ​ a → b \mathrm{a\,b}\,\ldots\,\mathrm{a}\rightarrow\mathrm{b} and its relabeling u ​ v ​ … ​ u → v \mathrm{u\,v}\,\ldots\,\mathrm{u}\rightarrow\mathrm{v} are solved by the same mechanism. A model that depends on this relational structure, rather than on specific token identities, is precisely the kind of model that generalizes to tokens unseen during tr

aining, which is a defining feature of in-context learning. We formalize this property as an invariance of the data distribution under a group of token relabelings. Consider a valid sequence of tokens 𝐱 ∈ 𝒱 N {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}} , where N = ∑ i = 1 n k i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}=\sum_{i=1}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}n}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}_{i} . Then, applying an admissible token permutation π ∈ S 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}\in S_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} —where S 𝒱 S_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} denote

s the symmetric group on 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} , i.e., the set of all bijections π : 𝒱 → 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} —should result in another valid sequence π ​ ( 𝐱 ) = π ​ ( 𝐱 1 ) ​ … ​ π ​ ( 𝐱 N ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}_{1})\ldots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,

0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}) . However, what counts as an admissible permutation? We define admissible token permutations based on the relational structure that a task imposes on the underlying vocabulary. Not every relabeling preserves the task: when the prediction relies on fixed relations between tokens, such as memorized associations, a permutation that does not respect those relations yields a different task. The admissible permutations are therefore exactly those that preserve this structure. Let ℛ ⊆ 𝒱 ρ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}}\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\rho}} be an ρ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\rho}} -ary relation—i.e., a set of ρ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\rho}} -tuples of tokens—characterizing basic skills required to solve the task. For instance, in the associative recall task, ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@g

ray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} is a set of associated token pairs (a binary relation, ρ = 2 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\rho}}=2 , formalized below); in a task whose context encodes memorized transitions of a Markov chain, ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} is the set of allowed transitions ( 𝐱 , 𝐱 ′ ) ({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}^{\prime}) . The relation ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} induces a subgroup of the symmetric group S 𝒱 S_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}} called the automorphism group of ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@g

ray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} ，其定义为 Aut ⁡ ( 𝒱 , ℛ ) := { π ∈ S 𝒱 : 𝐰 ∈ ℛ ⇔ π ​ ( 𝐰 ) ∈ ℛ } . \operatorname{Aut}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}}):=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}\in S_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}\;:\;\mathbf{w}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}}\Leftrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\pi}}(\mathbf{w})\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}}\}. (2) 其元素恰好是保持 ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} 的关系结构的排列。 为了简化本文余下部分的阐述，我们将 ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}

We show how our theory can be extended to arbitrarily complex task structures in appendix ˜ B . Positional Symmetries. In natural language, the relevant blocks [ 𝐱 1 i ​ ⋯ ​ 𝐱 k i i ] [{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}_{1}^{i}\cdots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}_{k_{i}}^{i}] may appear at any position in a document, separated by an arbitrary number of irrelevant words. We can simulate this effect by giving each block a random positional offset ϕ i ∈ ℕ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{

0}\phi}}_{i}\in\mathbb{N} , as if blocks were spread out in a very large hypothetical context. We can ensure that blocks don’t overlap by enforcing ϕ i + 1 ≥ ϕ i + k i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{i+1}\geq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{i}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}_{i} . Then, the positional embedding of the token 𝐱 j i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}^{i}_{j} is determined by its shifted position ϕ i + j {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{i}+j . This generalizes the encoding of positions and includes the special case with no offset: ϕ i + 1 = ϕ i + k i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{i+1}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{i}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}_{i} . 3 Invariant Manifolds of Learning Dynamics In this s

ection, we introduce our key theoretical contribution, the identification and characterization of the Invariant Manifold of Inductive Reasoning (IMIR) , a low-dimensional subspace to which parameters remain confined when trained via gradient descent on any block-list task. In section ˜ 3.1 , we describe the transformer architecture and training setup considered throughout the paper. In section ˜ 3.3 , we present our main theorem along with a brief proof sketch. In section ˜ 3.4 , we provide an interpretation of the IMIR’s structure. 3 3 3 We note that Corollary 1 establishes that the IMIR is invariant : a trajectory initialized on the IMIR remains on it throughout training. We do not prove the stronger property that the IMIR is attractive ; we only show this empirically in our experiments. 3.1 Transformer Architecture Our models are based on an attention-only transformer, which we describe here. Let N = ∑ i = 1 n k i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}=\sum_{i=1}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}n}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}_{i} denote the length of the input sequence. First, we map every token 𝐱 ∈ 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@co

lor@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} to an embedding vector 𝐞 𝐱 ∈ ℝ d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}} . Applying this embedding to our input sequence and target output, we obtain the model input 𝐓 ∈ ℝ d × N {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{T}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}} and output 𝐲 ∈ ℝ d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fil

l{0}\mathbf{y}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}}}} . Second, we use sinusoidal positional embeddings 𝐏 ∈ ℝ d × N {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{P}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}}}} , following Vaswani et al. [ 29 ] , with random block-level offsets as described in section ˜ 2.2 . As is standard, we then combine token and position embeddings to obtain the model inputs: 𝐇 ( 0 ) = 𝐓 + 𝐏 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(0)}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{T}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{P}}} . We pass these i

We train the model using the cross-entropy loss. We give a more detailed description of the architecture in section ˜ A.2 . Beyond Attention-Only Transformers. Standard Transformers additionally include feed-forward networks (FFN) and layer normalizations (LN). We omit these components from the core analysis in order to focus on attention layers, which are primarily responsible for i

nductive capabilities. Nevertheless, our theoretical results extend naturally to standard Transformers with FFNs and LNs, as we discuss in section ˜ A.5 . 3.2 Selection, Writing, and Action Bases Our main result ( Theorems ˜ 1 and 1 below) states that, when a transformer is trained on a block-list task, gradient descent confines its key–query and output weights to the span of a small set of basis matrices. In this section, we construct these basis matrices, each of which encodes one elementary action that is possibly useful for solving a block-list task. Our construction depends on three intermediate bases: selection matrices are learnable mappings of tokens or positions (e.g., “map each position to the previous”), writing bases track how each head writes information into the residual stream (and thus how later layers can read it back), and action bases track how chained attention heads transform the original embeddings (extending writing bases to multiple layers). When selecting contextual information, an attention head must compare transformed query and key representations. In our block-list task setup, this can be done in two ways: tokens can be compared directly to themselves via their identity, or they can be compared to their symmetry-related counterparts. These operations can be represented via the two matrices: 𝐈 ( t ) ∈ ℝ d × d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(t)}}}\i

n{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}}}} , the identity transformation acting only on the subspace of token embeddings, and 𝐂 ∈ ℝ d × d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}}}} , a projection matrix that maps the embeddings of symmetrically associated tokens to one another (associated via ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} ), mapping the embedding of each token 𝐚 i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}_{i} to the embedding of its associated partner 𝐛 i {{\color

Finally, each attention head may construct keys and queries using any of the representations that have accumulated up to that layer. This is achieved by rotating a selection matrix on the left side for the query and on the right side for the key, aligning the desired subspaces. Likewise, the final output layer may select any token representation by reading from the appropriate subspace. Similarly, each basis output weight in 𝒲 ¯ \bar{\mathcal{W}} reads a representation written by a subset of heads and maps it to an output token, either directly or through its association. 3.3 Invariant Dynamics Theory We now move forward to our main theorem. Before that, we highlight that our result relies on a few assumptions, which we state informally here and formally in section ˜ A.1 . First, we put forward the following data-related assumptions : (i) token associations in

ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} are fully interchangeable, so the set of valid inputs 𝒳 \mathcal{X} , where 𝐱 ∈ 𝒳 ⊆ 𝒱 N {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}\in\mathcal{X}\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{N} , is closed under association-preserving token permutations; (ii) block positions carry random offsets; and (iii) the embeddings of tokens which do not belong to any association in ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} , termed here rare tokens, are lexinvariant [ 30 ] (i.e., they are re-sampled for every sequence). We believe these assumptions to be straightforward and motivated by the traditional structure of block-list tasks. Second, we put forward the following architecture-related assumptions : (iv) token and position embeddings are orthonormal; (v) we analyze the merged key–query product 𝐖 Q ( ℓ ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 K ( ℓ ) {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell)}_{Q}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\

pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell)}_{K}}} as a single learnable matrix; and (vi) the output–value maps are fixed projections onto mutually orthogonal subspaces (following the associative-memory [ 17 ] and disentangled-residual-stream view [ 18 , 31 ] ). The extension of our results to models with learnable value projection matrices is sketched in appendices A.3.6 and A.4 . Theorem 1 (Gradient Confinement) . Consider the population-wise training dynamics of a model satisfying Assumptions ˜ 4 , 5 and 6 on a block-list task satisfying Assumptions ˜ 1 , 2 and 3 . W}}} be the tuple of all weights in this transformer. action over specific subspaces of embeddings. For attention layers, such interpretable actions might be, for example, “attending to the previous position” or “attending to the token retrieved by head 1”, while an output action might be something like “read the token retrieved by head 3”. When the model is on the IMIR, by decomposing it into its components corresponding to each weight matrix in 𝒲 ℓ \mathcal{W}_{\ell} and 𝒲 ¯ \bar{\mathcal{W}} , we can describe the model as a sum of highly interpretable actions. When the model is not on the IMIR, projecting it onto the IMIR provides a partial description. We give a more comprehensive and intuitive description of the weight spaces in section ˜ C.1 . We also give a theoretical motivation for the weight spaces from representational symmetries in section ˜ C.2 , discussing why they capture all actions relevant for inductive tasks. 4 Understanding Circuit Competition 4.1 Data Distributional Properties of Natural Language One important aspect not discussed above is how to sample the tokens 𝐱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}} used in the tasks. Notably, prior work [ 21 , 22 , 24 , 19 ] has shown that this choice can have a strong impact on the training dynamics of language models, and, in particular, that standard properties of token distributions in natural languages (e.g., Zipfian distributions or burstiness) can accelerate learning.

We incorporate a number of natural language distributional properties into our setup as follows: 1. Tokens differ in frequency. Token frequencies in natural language typically follow a long-tailed distribution [ 32 , 33 ] . To model this and bring our setup closer to natural language (while keeping it analytically tractable), we consider a subset of the tokens in our vocabulary to form a set of common tokens 𝒱 c {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{c}}} with total probability of occurrence ℙ ​ ( v ∈ 𝒱 c ) = 1 − f r \mathbb{P}(v\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{c}}})=1-f_{r} where f r ∈ [ 0 ; 1 ] f_{r}\in[0;1] . The remaining tokens are termed rare tokens : 𝒱 r = 𝒱 ∖ 𝒱 c {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{r}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gra

y@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}\setminus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{c}}} , with ℙ ​ ( v ∈ 𝒱 r ) = f r \mathbb{P}(v\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}^{r}}})=f_{r} . We assume that rare tokens form a virtually infinite set, where no rare token is ever observed twice. 7 7 7 In practice, we model such a virtually “infinite” vocabulary using a finite vocabulary and a lexinvariant assumption [ 30 ] , where a rare token’s embeddings are repeatedly re-sampled at every batch, inhibiting the model from memorizing it. 2. Tokens can be bursty. Documents in natural language tend to contain a concept or association multiple times, or not at all. In other words, in natural language, concepts do not show up uniformly, but rather in ‘bursts’. Consider, for example, the word ‘burstiness’ itself, which appears several times in this document, while most works do not contain it at all. We incorporate this behavior in our task class by assuming that block pairs present in the context have an average multiplicity of b ≥ 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{p

gfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}\geq 1 . For example, an input sequence with b = 2 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}=2 might look like " ab , gh , gh , ab , g → h \mathrm{ab,gh,gh,ab,g\rightarrow h} ". We write 𝒟 ​ ( b ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{D}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}) for the resulting data distribution at burstiness b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} , making the dependence explicit when we compare gradients across burstiness levels. 3. Tokens are polysemous. The same token can present different meanings and associations in different documents. For example, in computer science books, the name ‘Alan’ might be commonly followed by the surname ‘Turing’, but at times it might also be followed by a different surname such as ‘Baker’. 8 8 8 Alan Baker (1939–2018) was an English mathematician, known for his work on effective methods in number theory, in particular those arising from transcendental number theory. In our setup, we model this aspect by assuming that the next token to be predicted after a common token differs from its common associ

ation with frequency f v ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{v}}}\in[0,1] . Past work has shown that in-context learning abilities emerge optimally in transformers trained with many rare tokens, high burstiness, and high within-class variability [ 21 , 22 ] . In the absence of these properties, in-context learning emerges after significantly more training, or not at all. The same properties were shown to enable learning in a 3-parameter phenomenological model of induction-head formation [ 24 ] . Finally, burstiness was shown to accelerate the emergence of sparse attention in single-layer transformers [ 19 ] . We exemplify these properties in fig. ˜ 2 . 4.2 The Role of Data: In-Context Learning vs. In-Weights Learning We now use our Corollary ˜ 1 to study a very simple setting, but which already exhibits circuit competition: a 2-layer, single-head attention-only transformer trained to predict the second token of an in-context bigram. Together they thus implement in-context learning, the only solution that works on the rare part of the data. The fourth direction δ \delta accounts for in-weights learning, routing the input token to its canonical partner through a memorized association map 𝐂 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}} . Causal ablations confirming that ( α , β , γ ) (\alpha,\beta,\gamma) alone yields ICL and δ \delta alone yields IWL are reported in section ˜ D.2 . Figure 3: In two-layer transformers, ICL and IWL circuits compete during training. Top left: ICL has three circuit units ( α \alpha , β \beta , and γ \gamma , forming an induction head) and IWL has one circuit unit ( δ \delta , predicting the next token directly from the current token). Bottom left: the parameters become aligned with the IMIR during training. Center: the emergence of ICL/IWL abilities and circuits. Right: emergence of ICL/IWL during isolated training (only α \alpha , β \beta , γ \gamma , δ \delta ) exhibits similar dynamics. Training details in section ˜ D.1 . IWL emerges first a

The direction δ \delta is a one-dimensional fix that already solves every query answerable by a memorized canonical association—i.e., common-token queries without within-class variation, which make up a ( 1 − f r ) ​ ( 1 − f v ) (1-{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{r}}})(1-{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{v}}}) fraction of the data. It is the easiest direction for SGD to discover, empirically saturating well before ( α , β , γ ) (\alpha,\beta,\gamma) have moved appreciably ( fig. ˜ 3 , center). We give a detailed proof in section ˜ D.4 . ∎ Burstiness amplifies the ICL gradient. Theorem ˜ 2 describes how the rare-token frequency f r {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{r}}} and the within-class variability f v {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{v}}} reduce the gradient received by the ICL circuit. Burstiness, which we introduced in section ˜ 4.1 , has the opposite effect. In a bursty sequence, the block relevant to the query appears b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} times in the context, and each occ

urrence provides the same in-context evidence to the induction circuit, whereas the distractor blocks provide no consistent signal. The ICL gradient therefore grows with b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} , which accelerates the emergence of the induction head. Proof Sketch. Burstiness acts as a multiplier on the relevant blocks: the ICL gradient decomposes into per-block signals that are identical for the b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} relevant blocks and zero in expectation for distractors, so the relevant signal is multiplied by b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} . The only cross-block coupling is the shared layer- 2 2 softmax denominator Z q Z_{q} at the query position, which depends on b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} only through a relative O ​ ( b / n ) O({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\

pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}n}}) shift; since every per-block signal scales as 1 / Z q 1/Z_{q} , this yields a multiplicative ( 1 + O ​ ( b / n ) ) \big(1+O({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}n}})\big) correction rather than an additive one. This is crucial, because the leading term is itself of order b / n {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}n}} . We give a detailed proof in section ˜ D.5 . ∎ The induction head is a winning ticket. The Lottery Ticket Hypothesis (LTH) [ 25 ] posits that a randomly initialized network contains a sparse subnetwork which, trained in isolation, matches the dense network’s performance. Our framework identifies one such subnetwork explicitly: the four-parameter ( α , β , γ , δ ) (\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta) IMIR sub-manifold. We test the prediction directly via constrained training : at every SGD step we project the model back onto the subspace spanned by α , β , γ , δ \alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta . The resulting dynamics closely match those of the unconstrained network ( fig. ˜ 3 , right). 4.3 The Role of Initialization: Competing Inductio

n Heads Figure 4: Training a 3-layer Transformer to solve in-context bi-grams yields three different solutions. On the left, we show the winning solution at various initializations. On the right, we show the precise mechanism of each possible solution with formulas for model weights. An induction head can use any pair of attention heads in different layers. Thus, in a transformer with three layers and one head per layer, trained on the same task, three distinct induction-head circuits are possible, as depicted in fig. ˜ 4 (right). A randomly initialized model must converge to one of them (or a composition of them). To better understand how the initialization influences the learned circuit, we vary two parameters (denoted as β \beta and β ′′ \beta^{\prime\prime} ) and we plot the ‘winning’ circuit in fig. ˜ 4 (left). We give the full training details in section ˜ E.1 and we plot the full training dynamics for 12 seeds in section ˜ E.2 . Winning mechanics are complex to predict. In the LTH literature, winning subnetworks are typically identified by weight magnitude after training, and whether they can instead be predicted from properties of the initialization—such as initial weight magnitude—remains actively debated [ 34 , 35 , 36 ] . The simplest initialization-based predictor in our setup would be that each circuit wins when its own units start large. We can analyze this predictor by looking at fig. ˜ 4 (left). In fact, this predictor aligns with the fact that the ( α ′ , β ′

′ , γ ′ \alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime} )-circuit (layers 2 and 3, blue) tends to emerge when β ′′ \beta^{\prime\prime} is initialized with a large magnitude. However, the ( α , β ′ , γ ′ \alpha,\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime} )-circuit (layers 1 and 3, green) seems to benefit from a larger β \beta , which is not part of this circuit. Moreover, the ( α , β , γ \alpha,\beta,\gamma )-circuit (layers 1 and 2, red) seems to require not just a large β \beta (part of the circuit), but also a large β ′′ \beta^{\prime\prime} (not part of the circuit). Together, these results suggest the history is not that simple. The story of a circuit battle. A closer look at the phase transitions in fig. ˜ 4 (left) also sheds light on our transformer’s learning dynamics. First, note that these phase transitions are very sharp between the blue and green solutions and between blue and red, but not between green and red. This is because the red and green circuits are cooperative: both share α \alpha , while β \beta and β ′ \beta^{\prime} are in different layers. Meanwhile, blue is competing with both red and green, trying to use the same layers in different ways. This might also explain why red requires a large β ′′ \beta^{\prime\prime} : it stimulates the blue circuit, which in turn inhibits the green circuit. Notably, the learning dynamics plots in section ˜ E.2 appear to support this analysis. 5 Automated Circuit Detection for Induction Tasks Our theoretical framework offers tw

o benefits that may improve circuit discovery in trained models. First, the IMIR has a low dimensionality compared to the full parameter space, which greatly reduces the circuit search space. Second, while most interpretability works identify circuits at the level of attention heads [ 37 , 3 , 4 , 38 ] , the IMIR allows us to identify circuits at the more granular level of precise weight structures, enabling us to understand the exact mechanism of each head. 10 10 10 The bases present in our IMIR are also similar to the variables produced by the D-RASP decompilation procedure in Friedman et al. [ 31 ], Weiss et al. [ 39 ] . To illustrate these benefits, we apply our framework to the question of Automated Circuit Discovery [ 4 ] of induction circuits. We train deep attention-only single-head transformers on the two-hop induction task [ 10 ] (training details in section ˜ F.1 ). We discover the learned circuit using a simple algorithm: we project the learned parameters to the IMIR and we ablate the dimensions that don’t impact performance. We depict the recovered circuits schematically in fig. ˜ 5 . We describe the complete algorithm in section ˜ F.2 and the complete circuits in section ˜ F.3 . One interesting finding is that the attention paths depicted in fig. ˜ 5 are not implemented using only a minimal set of necessary directions, but also leverage a few additional ‘aiding’ directions. The additional directions marginally increase the accuracy by reducing the attention to i

rrelevant tokens. This kind of structure would be invisible to existing automated circuit discovery algorithms with granularity at the attention head level. Figure 5: Two auto-discovered circuits in deep Transformers for two-hop induction. 6 Conclusion and Future Work In this paper, we identified an invariant manifold for a class of transformers trained on block-list tasks. This manifold allows us to both study transformers’ training dynamics, and to decompose trained models’ weights into interpretable subspaces. We now discuss three directions for future work in which our theoretical framework might prove useful in addition to the use cases demonstrated in this paper. Improving Reasoning Abilities. Mechanistic interpretability has already proven useful for the design of improved neural network architectures [ 40 , 41 , 42 , 12 ] . For example, the selection mechanism in Mamba architectures [ 43 ] is inspired by induction heads [ 26 ] . A better understanding of inductive reasoning abilities (and their emergence) may thus enable the development of improved learning algorithms. In particular, our theoretical framework provides a geometric description of a large class of induction circuits. Useless circuits can be identified and pruned more effectively, while remaining circuits can be described using a reduced number of parameters, reducing the memory footprint and inference latency. Moreover, it is known that learning induction heads is more difficult in long sequences [ 20 ]

, while deep circuits require either an implicit curriculum or an exponential amount of data [ 11 , 44 ] . Can we design neural architectures without these limitations? Accelerating Experiments at Scale. In deep learning, many important phenomena, such as data dependency of ICL [ 21 ] or generalization beyond overfitting [ 45 ] , began as empirical observations in synthetic tasks, before they were later shown to hold in general settings [ 22 , 46 ] . There is a simple reason for this: training large models on full datasets is slow and costly, whereas uncovering novel phenomena may require running a great number of experiments quickly. Our framework can further accelerate experimentation in two ways. First, by constraining learning to the IMIR, or even a reduced subset of interesting dimensions, training can be greatly optimized, reducing experiment time and computational demands. Second, by providing the geometric description of meaningful directions, our framework can aid hypothesis generation and understanding of training dynamics. Invariant Manifolds in Other Tasks. In this work, we have shown how invariant manifolds can be used to understand training dynamics and model internals. While invariant manifolds of learning dynamics have been studied before [ 47 , 48 , 20 ] , to the best of our knowledge, this work is the first to identify the geometry of the invariant manifolds responsible for learning a large class of tasks. If inductive tasks exhibit a low-dimensional invaria

nt manifold, then maybe other tasks also do. Uncovering them could lead to a better understanding of learning dynamics in deep neural networks. References Zheng et al. Appendix A Details of the theoretical results A.1 Overview of the assumptions Theorem ˜ 1 (and its Corollary ˜

1 ) holds under the six assumptions stated formally below, each followed by its motivation. The first three constrain the data distribution ( Assumptions ˜ 1 , 3 and 4 ); the last three fix the architecture and parameterization we analyze ( Assumptions ˜ 5 and 6 ). The same assumptions are summarized informally in words just above Theorem ˜ 1 . Assumption 2 (Independent positional offsets) . Each block carries an independent positional offset ϕ j , b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{j,b} , drawn i.i.d. and marginally uniform, so t

hat the data distribution is unchanged under a global per-frequency offset shift ϕ j , b ↦ ϕ j , b + Δ ​ ϕ j {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{j,b}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{j,b}+\Delta{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}_{j} . Our data uses these random offsets to simulate the presence of an arbitrary number of irrelevant tokens that may separate consecutive blocks in a real document. d]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{()}_{Q}}},\mathbf{R}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{()}_{K}}}) and is exactly what the basis weights 𝒲 ℓ \mathcal{W}_{\ell} describe. Relaxing it to independently learnable factors is discussed in section ˜ A.4 . Assumption 6 (Fixed orthogonal output–value maps) . Each output–value map 𝐕 ( ℓ , h ) = 𝐖 P ( ℓ , h ) ​ 𝐖 V ( ℓ , h ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell,h)}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell,h)}_{P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell,h)}_{V}}} is a fixed isometry onto a write subspace that is orthogonal to the residual stream entering layer ℓ \ell and to the write subspaces of the other heads of layer ℓ \ell , so that 𝐕 ( ℓ , h ) ⊺ ​ 𝐕 ( ℓ , h ′ ) = δ h ​ h ′ ​ 𝐈 {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell,h)}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell,h^{\prime

})}}}=\delta_{hh^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}} on that input space. This is the multi-head form of the disentangled residual stream [ 31 , 18 ] , again justified by the associative-memory view: large random projections write into near-orthogonal subspaces. It is the main simplification of our analysis. The detailed proof in section ˜ A.3 establishes the theorem under it, and section ˜ A.4 sketch (without a complete proof) how learnable value and projection factorizations might be accommodated. A.2 Transformer Architecture In this section, we recap our transformer architecture in a bit more detail. First, we denote our model’s vocabulary as 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} , and we map every token 𝐱 ∈ 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} to a non-learnable embedding vector 𝐞 𝐱 ∈ ℝ d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}

_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}} . We use Embed : 𝒱 → ℝ d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathrm{Embed}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}} to denote the mapping Embed ​ [ 𝐱 ] = 𝐞 𝐱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathrm{Embed}}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}]}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@strok

e{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}} . Applying this embedding to our input sequence and target output, we obtain the inputs 𝐓 ∈ ℝ d × N {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{T}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}} and output 𝐲 ∈ ℝ d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{y}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}}}} . As discussed above, we assume each block can be separated by an arbitrary number of irrelevant words, which we model by adding random offsets for each block. (18) A.3 Proof of Theorem ˜ 1 See 1 Proof. The proof proceeds in six steps. First ( section ˜ A.3.1 ), we set up the objects the argument acts on: the orthogonal token and position embedding subspaces 𝕌 ( t ) , 𝕌 ( p ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(t)}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(p)}}}} , the selection matrices 𝒯 , 𝒫 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{T}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{P}}} that generate the basis weights, and the representation spaces reachable at each layer. Second ( section ˜ A.3.2 ), we isolate a simplified single-head subspace with identity queries and fixed orthogonal value matrices, which already exposes the mechanism; the learnable, multi-head case is recovered at the end. Third ( section ˜ A.3.3 ), we encode the two data symmetries o

f a block-list task (permuting associated common tokens and shifting block positional offsets) as a pair of orthogonal rotations acting on 𝕌 ( t ) , 𝕌 ( p ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(t)}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(p)}}}} . Fourth ( section ˜ A.3.4 ), we show that these rotations leave all attention scores invariant, so the hidden representations and the model output are simply rotated by the same transformation; consequently the loss is unchanged. Fifth ( section ˜ A.3.5 ), we propagate this equivariance backward to find how each weight’s gradient transforms, and observe that, because the data distribution is invariant, the population gradient must itself be invariant under the transformation. Sixth ( section ˜ A.3.6 ), we analyze which matrices can be invariant: a commutant argument forces every gradient block to vanish across concepts and, within a concept, to be a polynomial in the selection matrices, exactly the basis-weight structure span ​ ( 𝒲 ℓ ) , span ​ ( 𝒲 ¯ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathrm{span}}}(\mathcal{W}_{\ell}),{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\ma

thrm{span}}}(\bar{\mathcal{W}}) defining 𝒮 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{S}}} . This establishes 𝔼 ​ [ ∇ 𝐖 ℒ ] ∈ 𝒮 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{E}}}[\nabla_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}]\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{S}}} and hence Theorem ˜ 1 . ∎ We note that in section ˜ A.4 we then lift our argument to independently learnable factorizations via circuit folding. A.3.2 Proving the Invariance of a Simplified Space We will prove the invariance of a simplified transformer where: (i) only the key matrices 𝐖 K ( ℓ , h ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell,h)}_{K}}} and the output matrix 𝐖 ¯ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\bar{\mathbf{W}}}} are learnable and the others are considered fixed orthogonal matrices; (ii) with a single attention head per layer ( H = 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}H}}=1 ); and (iii) full-dimension head ( d h = d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d_{h}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}} ). Extending the proof to multi-head attention is straightforward, and we later informally sketch a ‘circuit folding’ technique that generalizes our proof to a larger class of invariant spaces with arbitrary d h {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d_{h

}}} and where all matrices are learnable. For this simplified calculation thus, we consider full-dimension single-head attention layers ( H = 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}H}}=1 and d h = d {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d_{h}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}d}} ) and we take the query matrix to be an identity matrix 𝐖 Q ( ℓ , 1 ) = 𝐈 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell,1)}_{Q}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}} . (Note that this is mathematically equivalent to considering a merged key–query matrix.) ecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell,1)}_{V}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{(\ell-1)}}} . olor@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}\;+\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell)}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\sigma\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}}^{\intercal}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell)}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}\right)}, (22) These simplifications are in line with the associative memory view of Bietti et al. A.3.3 Input Transformations We now define two likelihood-preserving rotations to the token and position embeddings. The two coincide in this simplified setup where the fixed 𝐕 ( ℓ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell)}}} are isometries onto orthogonal subspaces, i.e. 𝐕 + = 𝐕 ⊺ \mathbf{V}^{+}={\mathbf{V}}^{\intercal} . Attention scores are unchanged. We now aim

to establish that attention scores remain unchanged under these transformations. A.3.5 Backward Pass We now backward propagate through our model, and show how the previous transformation affects our gradients. We also show here that the population gradients are preserved under such a transformation. Transformed Gradients. We start by the final softmax and unembedding layers. en different positions is not affected by a global offset change. Since the attention scores are invariant to our transformations, their gradients are also invariant. (39) A.3.6 Gradient Blocks Finally, in this section we conclude our proof that our model’s gradient will live inside the constructed manifold. To that end, we first analyze which matrices are invariant with respect to the studied transfo

rmations. Rather than analyzing the gradient directly, we decompose it into blocks indexed by pairs of concepts (tokens or positions) and pairs of layer subsets, i.e., by the possible interaction paths through the network. This decomposition is convenient because each block is easier to treat in isolation, and all blocks follow the same symmetry argument. Moreover, it loses nothing: the residual-stream activations are supported entirely on the concept subspaces and their images under the composite value maps, so any gradient block not considered below is zero, since the corresponding activations are zero. For a subset of layers S ⊆ [ L ] S\subseteq[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}L}}] , we define the product of the corresponding value matrices, taken in descending layer order (later layers on the left), matching the composites in 𝒱 1 : L {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}_{1:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}L}}}}} : 𝐕 ( S ) = ∏ ℓ ∈ S 𝐕 ( ℓ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(S)}}}\;=\;\prod_{\ell\;\in\,S}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@co

r}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}}\mathbf{G}^{(t)}\quad\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{O}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(c)}}}}). (44) We can analyze these two constraints using standard linear algebra and symmetry properties. Token Subspace. The transformation 𝐄 ( t ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}} represents permutations of common tokens that strictly preserve associations (e.g., swapping one pair of associated tokens with another, or swapping tokens within a pair). Let us view 𝐆 ( t ) \mathbf{G}^{(t)} as a weighted adjacency matrix connecting tokens in 𝕌 ( c ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(c)}}}} . For 𝐆 ( t ) \mathbf{G}^{(t)} to commute with all valid permutations 𝐄 ( t ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pg

fstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}} , the weights must be symmetric and uniform across all interchangeable token relationships. Because any token pair can be swapped with any other pair, the diagonal entries of 𝐆 ( t ) \mathbf{G}^{(t)} (self-connections) must all be identical. Similarly, because tokens within a pair can be swapped, the entries connecting a token to its associated pair must all be identical. Any connection between tokens belonging to different pairs would break the permutation symmetry unless all such connections were identical. Therefore, 𝐆 ( t ) \mathbf{G}^{(t)} must have all entries equal except for the diagonal and the off-diagonal entries corresponding to valid associations, i.e. 𝐆 ( t ) = c 1 ​ 𝟏 ( t ) + c 2 ​ 𝐈 ( t ) + c 3 ​ 𝐂 \mathbf{G}^{(t)}=c_{1}\mathbf{1}^{(t)}+c_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(t)}}}+c_{3}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}} . The bias term 𝟏 ( t ) \mathbf{1}^{(t)} of the gradient vanishes ( c 1 ≈ 0 c_{1}\approx 0 ) when all tokens have the same L1 norm in the basis of common tokens. In our setup, all common tokens have the same L2 norm (i.e. ‖ 𝐞 𝐱 ‖ 2 = const. \|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gra

y@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}}\|_{2}=\text{const.} ), which ensures equal L1 norm in the token basis. Furthermore, lex-invariant rare token embeddings are generated with the same mean and variance as common tokens, which ensures nearly-equal L1 norm when the dimensionality is large. Positional Subspace. The transformation 𝐄 ( p ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(p)}}} represents a continuous shift in the positional offsets. By definition of our sinusoidal embeddings, 𝕌 ( p ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(p)}}}} is spanned by orthogonal 2D subspaces corresponding to the fundamental frequencies. In this basis, any rotation 𝐄 ( p ) ​ ( Δ ​ ϕ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(p)}}}(\Delta{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}) is a block-diagonal matrix where each 2 × 2 2\times 2 block is a standard 2D rotation matrix. For 𝐆 ( p ) \mathbf

{G}^{(p)} to commute with 𝐄 ( p ) ​ ( Δ ​ ϕ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(p)}}}(\Delta{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}}) for all continuous values of Δ ​ ϕ \Delta{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\phi}} , standard matrix algebra dictates that 𝐆 ( p ) \mathbf{G}^{(p)} must also be block-diagonal in the exact same basis, and its 2 × 2 2\times 2 blocks must be scaled 2D rotation matrices themselves. Recall that we considered that all frequencies have order k {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}} , i.e. ω j = 2 ​ π ​ N j / k {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\omega}}_{j}=2\pi N_{j}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}} , but there are fundamentally only k {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}} such distinct frequencies. Permuting the offsets of equivalent frequencies preserves the output and loss of the model, hence the gradient sub-block rotations correspon

ding to equivalent frequencies must be equal. Therefore, the gradient contains at most k {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}} distinct sub-blocks, each of which is a 2 × 2 2\times 2 scaled rotation. A polynomial of order k {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}} in 𝐌 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}} (i.e., 𝐆 ( p ) = c 0 ​ 𝐈 ( p ) + c 1 ​ 𝐌 + … + c k − 1 ​ 𝐌 k − 1 \mathbf{G}^{(p)}=c_{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(p)}}}+c_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}+\ldots+c_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}-1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}-1} ) requires one additional constraint: sub-blocks corresponding to opposite frequencies (i.e., ω \omega and ω ′ = 2 ​ π − ω \omega^{\prime}=2\pi-\omega ) must b

e the transpose of each other (i.e., scaled rotations with the same scaling factors but opposite rotations). To establish this, we must examine the structure of the gradient in more detail. Note that 𝐆 ( p ) \mathbf{G}^{(p)} is the gradient block of an attention matrix 𝐖 ( ℓ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell)}}} , which is obtained as: ∂ ℒ ∂ 𝐖 ( ℓ ) = 𝐇 ( ℓ − 1 ) ​ ∂ ℒ ∂ A ​ 𝐇 ( ℓ − 1 ) ⊺ , \displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\frac{\partial{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}}{\partial{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell)}}}}}}\;=\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\frac{\partial{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}}{\partial A}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\ma

(45) In particular, our gradient block will be 𝐆 ( p ) = 𝔼 ​ [ ∑ i ​ j ( ∂ ℒ / ∂ A i ​ j ) ​ p i ​ p j ⊺ ] \mathbf{G}^{(p)}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{E}}}[{\sum}_{ij}(\partial{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}/\partial A_{ij})\,p_{i}{p_{j}}^{\intercal}] , where p i , p j ∈ 𝕌 ( p ) p_{i},p_{j}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(p)}}}} are the positional components at two locations i , j ∈ [ n ​ k ] i,j\in[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}

\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}n}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}] in the sequence. Since attention score gradients ∂ ℒ / ∂ A i ​ j \partial{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}/\partial A_{ij} are shared across frequencies, it suffices to focus on a single attention path. Isolating the 2 × 2 2\times 2 gradient sub-block corresponding to a frequency ω \omega , we get 𝐆 i ​ j ​ ω = 𝔼 ​ [ p ω ​ i ​ p ω ​ j ⊺ ] \mathbf{G}_{ij\omega}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{E}}}[p_{\omega i}{p_{\omega j}}^{\intercal}] . By combining the structural constraints of both the token and p

ositional subspaces, we recover the desired geometry for all gradient blocks. Multi-Head Attention. The above proof can be easily extended to multi-head attention: 𝐇 ( ℓ ) = 𝐇 ( ℓ − 1 ) + ∑ h = 1 H 𝐕 ( ℓ , h ) ​ 𝐇 ( ℓ − 1 ) ​ σ ​ ( 𝐇 ( ℓ − 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 ( ℓ , h ) ​ 𝐇 ( ℓ − 1 ) ) . {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell)}}}\;=\;\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}\;+\;\sum_{h=1}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}H}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell,h)}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\sigma\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}^{(\ell-1)}}}}^{\intercal}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(\ell,h)}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{r
