Looking at the gradient blocks indexed by concepts and sets of heads, the desired structure can be established using identical arguments. A.4 Circuit Folding: Invariant Dynamics with Interacting Subspaces Theorem ˜ 1 assumes fixe

d orthogonal output–value maps ( Assumption ˜ 6 ); relaxing this is more delicate, and we record the extension here as a sketch rather than a proven claim. Allowing the value maps 𝐕 ( ℓ , h ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{V}^{(\ell,h)}}} to be learned lets concept subspaces rotate or mix, and allowing the key–query and output–value factorizations to be learned independently (rather than only through their merged products, Assumption ˜ 5 ) requires tracking the intermediate representations rather than just those products. We expect the same block-level arguments to extend to factorizations in which subspaces interact only within a single concept (tokens or positions) and are transformed only by their fundamental actions; this appendix sets up the circuit-folding formalism needed to make this precise. We emphasize that this is a sketch, and leave a complete treatment to future work. To illustrate the idea, consider any learnable matrix 𝐖 ∈ ℝ d 2 × d 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{1}} of the network. For any orthogonal input and output concept spaces, we can establish the following invariant manifold: 𝐖 ∈ span ​ ( ℬ t o ⊺ ​ 𝒯 ​ ℬ t i + ℬ p o ⊺ ​ 𝒫 ​ ℬ p i ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0

}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}}\;\in\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathrm{span}}}({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{t}^{o}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{T}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{t}^{i}}}+{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{p}^{o}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{p}^{i}}}) (47) where ℬ t i ⊂ ℝ d t × d 1 , ℬ t o ⊂ ℝ d t × d 2 , ℬ p i ⊂ ℝ d p × d 1 , ℬ p o ⊂ ℝ d p × d 2 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{t}^{i}}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{t}\times d_{1}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{t}^{o}}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{t}\times

d_{2}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{p}^{i}}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{p}\times d_{1}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{B}_{p}^{o}}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{p}\times d_{2}} denote the sets of orthogonal basis vectors for the orthogonal input/output token/position spaces. Consider a sequence of representations 𝐇 ∈ ℝ d i × n {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{H}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}\times n} that we transform by applying the same input transformation as before. A.5 Beyond Attention Only: Feed-Forward Networks & Layer Norm We extend Corollary ˜ 1 to standard Transformer layers, which interleave multi-head attention with two further operations: position-wise feed-forward networks (FFN) and layer normalization (LN). Both operations commute with the embedding-space symmetries used in section ˜ A.3 , provided their parameters fit the natural extension of the IMIR structure described below. The argument is the same in spirit as the multi-head extension of section ˜ A.3.6 : one writes down a global residual-stream rotation Λ ( ℓ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\Lambda^{(\ell)}}} , checks that the new operation commutes with it, and propagates the block-level analysis through the new gradient blocks. We adopt the notation of section ˜ A.3 : 𝐄 ( t ) ∈ 𝒪 ​ ( 𝕌 ( c ) ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{O}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(c)}}

}}) is the orthogonal map induced by an association-preserving permutation of common tokens, 𝐄 ( p ) ∈ 𝒪 ​ ( 𝕌 ( p ) ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(p)}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{O}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(p)}}}}) is the rotation on the position subspace induced by a global offset shift, and Λ ( ℓ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\Lambda^{(\ell)}}} is the composite rotation on the residual stream at layer ℓ \ell . Layer Normalization. hbf{g},\mathbf{b}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}} are learnable gain and bias vectors. Two facts make LN compatible with Λ ( ℓ − 1 ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\Lambda^{(\ell-1)}}} . 1. Both 𝐄 ( t ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(t)}}} and 𝐄 ( p ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(p)}}} act on 𝕌 ( c ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(c)}}}} and 𝕌 ( p ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{(p)}}}} respectively, and these subspaces are orthogonal to 𝟏 \mathbf{1} in our setup (common-token and sinusoidal position embeddings are zero-mean by construction). {0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{E}^{(p)}}} ) implies that the expected gradient blocks are polynomials in the fundamental actions and therefore fit the prescribed FFN-IMIR structure. Putting it together. Inserting any number of FFN and LN layers, in any order, between the attention layers does not break Corollary ˜ 1 . The extended manifold consists of the original attention-weight constraints of section ˜ A.3 , plus the FFN-weight constraints above, plus the LN gain/bias constraints. The forward pass commutes with Λ ( ℓ ) {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\Lambda^{(\ell)}}} at every layer (attention, FFN, or LN); the loss is therefore invariant under our embedding rotations; the expected gradient is invariant too; and the same block-by-block argument forces each gradient block — attention, FFN, or LN — into the prescribed polynomial form. The full Transformer thus retains the same low-dimensional invariant manifold as the attention-only model. Appendix B Arbitrary Relational Structure In the main text we specialized the discussion to irreflexive symmetric binary relations, which already captures the bulk of the inductive tasks studied in the literature. The framework, however, applies to arbitrary relational structures on the vocabulary. In this appendix we describe the general construction and then work out the directed pairwise case as a concrete exte

nsion. The two ingredients are always the same: a relation ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} induces a token symmetry group, the symmetry group induces orthogonal transformations on representations, and the corresponding commutant pins down the admissible operators that learning dynamics can produce. First, the identity 𝐈 = 𝐈 A + 𝐈 B + 𝐈 D {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}_{A}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}_{B}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}_{D} splits into separate role projectors, so attention heads can now condition on whether a token plays the role of a source, of a target, or of an unrelated filler. Second, the symmetric association operator 𝐂 = 𝐂 A → B + 𝐂 B → A {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{

0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}_{A\to B}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}_{B\to A} of the main text splits into two oriented operators, allowing the circuit algebra to encode directional relations such as 𝐚 → 𝐛 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}} without collapsing onto its inverse. Before centering, the full commutant additionally contains uniform coupling operators of the form 𝐉 R ​ S := ∑ r ∈ R ∑ s ∈ S 𝐞 r ​ 𝐞 s ⊺ \mathbf{J}_{RS}:=\sum_{r\in R}\sum_{s\in S}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{r}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{s}}}}^{\intercal} , for role types R , S ∈ { A , B , D } R,S\in\{A,B,D\} . These operators correspond to nonzero mean directions of the symmetry classes; centering the embeddings within each class removes them, and the local algebra above is recovered as the essential symmetry-compatible structure. The construction of the basis weights 𝒲 ℓ \mathcal{W}_{\ell} in section ˜ 3.3 then proceeds verbatim, with the token sele

ction set 𝒯 = { 𝐈 ( t ) , 𝐂 } {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{T}}}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(t)}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}\} replaced by the directed selection set { 𝐈 A , 𝐈 B , 𝐈 D , 𝐂 A → B , 𝐂 B → A } \{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}_{A},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}_{B},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}}}_{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}_{A\to B},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}}_{B\to A}\} , and the invariant manifold theorem ( Corollary ˜ 1 ) carries over without further change. B.4 General Recipe The two examples treated so far – symmetric pairwise relations in the main text and directed pairwise relations above – follow the same recipe, and the recipe exte

nds to arbitrary ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} : 1. Compute the automorphism group Aut ⁡ ( 𝒱 , ℛ ) \operatorname{Aut}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}}) of the relation, and partition 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} into orbits of role-equivalent tokens. 2. Com

pute the commutant 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒰 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{U}) . Its dimension equals the number of orbits of Aut ⁡ ( 𝒱 , ℛ ) \operatorname{Aut}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}}) acting on 𝒱 × 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} , and a basis is given by the orbit-indicator operators ∑ ( 𝐱 , 𝐱 ′ ) ∈ 𝒪 𝐞 𝐱 ​ 𝐞 𝐱 ′ ⊺ \sum_{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{O}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[nam

ed]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}^{\prime}}}}}^{\intercal} , indexed by orbits 𝒪 \mathcal{O} . 4. Use 𝒞 ​ ( 𝒰 ) \mathcal{C}(\mathcal{U}) as the token selection set in place of 𝒯 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{T}}} and form the basis weights 𝒲 ℓ \mathcal{W}_{\ell} as in section ˜ 3.3 . For binary ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} defining a directed graph, this recipe identifies the isomorphic connected components of the graph; the symmetric pair and the directed pair are the simplest such components. For higher-arity relations the orbit count grows accordingly, and the commutant captures progressively richer relational structure (such as equivalence classes, n n -cycles, or transition kernels). In every case

the same gradient-invariance argument applies and the corresponding basis weights remain invariant under population gradient flow. Appendix C The Geometry of Reasoning Circuits In this appendix, we discuss the geometry of the Invariant Manifold of Inductive Reasoning (IMIR) in more detail. We first give an intuitive description of its coordinate frame, and then we give a theoretical motivation for its origin based on representational symmetries. C.1 What is an Attention Head? Now that our key theoretical result has been established, we give an intuitive description of its geometry. Using a toy attention example, we introduce the mechanisms of the circuits that exist in the Invariant Manifold of Inductive Reasoning. Consider the input embeddings 𝐡 i 0 = 𝐞 𝐚 + 𝐩 i \mathbf{h}^{0}_{i}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}}}}+\mathbf{p}_{i} (where 𝐡 i 0 ∈ ℝ d \mathbf{h}^{0}_{i}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{R}^{d}}} ) of token 𝐚 ∈ 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke

{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} at position i ∈ [ N ] i\in[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}] . What can an attention head do with this input? Token Actions. In our setup, we consider that the only correlations present in the data are between non-overlapping pairs of associated tokens in ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} , as formalized by the ℛ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} -invariance assumption of section ˜ A.1 . Thus, for each token, the model can learn only two possible mappings: to the same token or to the associated token. Applying this to attention heads, we get two possible token actions. First, an attention head may look for the exact token it has, constructing key and query vectors that align when the input tokens match, i.e. 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐞 𝐚 ≈ 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐞 𝐛 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}

\mathbf{a}}}}}}\approx{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}} when 𝐚 = 𝐛 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}} . This is achieved when the key-query circuit is approximately a multiple of the identity transformation over the subspace of token embeddings, i.e. 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ≈ α ​ 𝐈 ( t ) {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\approx\alpha{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(t)}}} , where 𝐈 ( t ) = ∑ 𝐱 ∈ 𝒱 𝐞 𝐱 ​ 𝐞 𝐱 ⊺ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke

{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(t)}}}=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{x}}}}}}}}}^{\intercal} . The second possible action for the attention head is to look for the associated token, i.e. 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐞 𝐚 ≈ 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐞 𝐛 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fil

l{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}}}}\approx{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}} when { 𝐚 , 𝐛 } ∈ ℛ \{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}\}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{R}}} . In this case, the key-query circuit must act as a mapping between associated tokens, i.e. 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ≈ α ​ 𝐂 {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rg

b}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\approx\alpha{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{C}}} , 其中 𝐂 = ∑ { 𝐚 , 𝐛 } ∈ ℛ ( 𝐞 𝐚 ​ 𝐞

lor@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}}}}}^{\intercal}) . Position Actions. In our setup, each block has a random positional offset, simulating an arbitrary (unknown) number of imaginary tokens separating consecutive blocks. This breaks positional correlations between separate blocks, which means that positional embeddings may only be used to attend to a previous position in the same block (up to k − 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}-1 positions back). Indeed, since block offsets are sampled independently, the relative position between tokens of different blocks is uniformly random and carries no usable information. This is achieved by constructing key and query vectors that align for a certain position distance, i.e. 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ​ p i ≈ 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ​ p i − j {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}\,\textbf{p}_{i}\approx{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfs

ys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\,\textbf{p}_{i-j} for a distance j ∈ [ k − 1 ] j\in[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}k}}-1] . Using translation-invariant sinusoidal embeddings, this is achieved when the key-query circuit encodes a specific rotation 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ≈ α ​ 𝐌 j {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\approx\alpha{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}^{j} , where 𝐌 = 𝔼 ​ [ 𝐩 i − 1 ​ 𝐩 i ⊺ ] {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{E}}}[\mathbf{p}_{i-1}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\intercal}] and 𝐌 j {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}^{j} denotes the j j -th matrix power of 𝐌 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stro

ke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}} . The power arises because 𝐌 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}} rotates every frequency plane by its unit-position angle, mapping each position embedding to that of the preceding position ( 𝐌 ​ 𝐩 i = 𝐩 i − 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}\,\mathbf{p}_{i}=\mathbf{p}_{i-1} ); applying it j j times thus shifts positions back by j j , i.e. 𝐌 j ​ 𝐩 i = 𝐩 i − j {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{M}}}^{j}\,\mathbf{p}_{i}=\mathbf{p}_{i-j} . Deep circuits might also benefit from attending to the exact same position using 𝐖 K ( 1 , 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 Q ( 1 , 1 ) ≈ α ​ 𝐈 ( p ) {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{K}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{Q}}}\approx\alpha{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(p)}}} , where 𝐈 ( p ) = 𝔼 ​ [ 𝐩 i ​ 𝐩 i ⊺ ] {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsy

s@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(p)}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbb{E}}}[\mathbf{p}_{i}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\intercal}] . To see how this extends to stacked attention heads, assume that the first attention head has attended entirely to a token 𝐛 ∈ 𝒱 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}} at position j ∈ [ N ] j\in[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}N}}] . The residual stream at position i i has become 𝐡 i 1 = 𝐞 𝐚 + 𝐩 i + 𝐖 P ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐖 V ( 1 , 1 ) ​ ( 𝐞 𝐛 + 𝐩 j ) \mathbf{h}^{1}_{i}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}}}}+\mathbf{p}_{i}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{V}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0

}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}}+\mathbf{p}_{j}) . How could a second attention head leverage these representations? Output-Value Projections. While the previously described actions remain perfectly valid, a whole new range of possibilities is unlocked. A second head can now attend using the information retrieved by the first head. For example, the second head can attend to positions containing the token retrieved by the first head if 𝐖 K ( 2 , 1 ) ​ ( 𝐖 P ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐖 V ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐞 𝐛 ) ≈ 𝐖 Q ( 2 , 1 ) ​ 𝐞 𝐛 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(2,1)}_{K}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{V}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}})\approx{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecol

or}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(2,1)}_{Q}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}} or 𝐖 K ( 2 , 1 ) ⊺ ​ 𝐖 Q ( 2 , 1 ) ≈ 𝐖 P ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐖 V ( 1 , 1 ) ​ 𝐈 ( t ) {{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(2,1)}_{K}}}}^{\intercal}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(2,1)}_{Q}}}\approx{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{P}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}^{(1,1)}_{V}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{I}^{(t)}}} . The information retrieved by the first head can also be used by the second head when constructing queries. There are twice as many ways to construct keys and queries, so the number of total possible behaviors has increased four-fold. Combined Projections. Note that the residual stream is now a sum of eight embedding terms — the four token embeddings 𝐞 𝐚 , 𝐞 𝐛 , 𝐞 𝐚 ′ , 𝐞 𝐛 ′ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{a}}}^{\prime}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{e}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{b}}}^{\prime}}}} and the four position embeddings 𝐩 i , 𝐩 j , 𝐩 i

′ , 𝐩 j ′ \mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{p}_{j},\mathbf{p}_{i^{\prime}},\mathbf{p}_{j^{\prime}} — each rotated into its own subspace by the output–value projections of the heads that wrote it. Each of them could be used by a third head to construct queries and keys by accessing the corresponding subspaces. 11 11 11 The number of combined projections across layers is analogous to the number of possible code calls when converting a transformer into D-RASP, a programming language proposed to abstract transformers [ 49 ] . C.2 Representational Symmetry of Inductive Tasks Token Embeddings. We use a data representation with fixed, non-learnable embeddings, mapping every 𝐱 ∈ 𝒱 \mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V} to a vector 𝐞 𝐱 ∈ ℝ d \mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , resulting in the embedding matrix 𝐄 ∈ ℝ m × d \mathbf{E}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d} . However, in the context of induction head learning, the model should not memorize specific embeddings in its parameters. Rather, it should learn to associate tokens with certain roles, as is the case in the class of block-list tasks described above. Hence, we want the model weights to be invariant under transformation symmetries S S of the data representation 𝐄 \mathbf{E} : What is learned with 𝐄 \mathbf{E} should generalize to all input representations 𝐄 ′ = 𝐔𝐄 \mathbf{E}^{\prime}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{E} , with 𝐔 ∈ S \mathbf{U}\in S . Specifically, natural transformation symmetries to consider are subgroups of orthogonal transformations S ⊆ O ​ ( d

) S\subseteq O(d) . As is well established, such orthogonal invariances can be tied to the commutant of the symmetry group 𝒞 ​ ( S ) := { 𝐖 : 𝐖𝐔 = 𝐔𝐖 , ∀ 𝐔 ∈ S } . \displaystyle\mathcal{C}(S):=\{\mathbf{W}:\mathbf{W}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W},\;\forall\,\mathbf{U}\in S\}\,. (69) It can be shown easily that, for instance, equivariance of linear operators and invariance of bi-linear operators constrains them to be in 𝒞 ​ ( S ) \mathcal{C}(S) . The larger S S , the more constrained are operators, for instance, 𝒞 ​ ( O ​ ( d ) ) = span ⁡ ( 𝐈 ) \mathcal{C}(O(d))=\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{I}) and invariant operators can only re-scale data. What is then a suitable choice for S S ? Our focus is on retaining associative pairwise relations between vocabulary items. Hence, we assume that the task implies a symmetric, irreflexive binary relation ∼ \sim on 𝒱 \mathcal{V} such that each element is related to exactly one other element (a perfect matching). This induces a subgroup S ⊆ O ​ ( d ) S\subseteq O(d) consisting of permutation matrices 𝐔 π \mathbf{U}_{\pi} corresponding to permutations π \pi of 𝒱 \mathcal{V} that preserve the relation, i.e. 𝐱 ∼ 𝐲 ⟺ π ​ ( 𝐱 ) ∼ π ​ ( 𝐲 ) \mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{y}\Longleftrightarrow\pi(\mathbf{x})\sim\,\pi(\mathbf{y}) . The commutation relation implies that operators in 𝒞 ​ ( S ) \mathcal{C}(S) are constant on orbits of index pairs under S S . There are three such orbits 𝒪 1 = { ( 𝐱 , 𝐱 ) : 𝐱 ∈ 𝒱 } , 𝒪 2 = { ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) : 𝐱 ∼ 𝐲 , 𝐱 , 𝐲 ∈ 𝒱 } , 𝒪 3

= 𝒪 ¯ 1 ∩ 𝒪 ¯ 2 . \displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{1}=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\},\quad\mathcal{O}_{2}=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbf{y},\;\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}\},\quad\mathcal{O}_{3}=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\cap\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\,. (70) and the commutant is given by 𝒞 ​ ( S ) = span ⁡ { 𝐈 , 𝐂 , 𝟏𝟏 ⊤ } , 𝐂 := 𝟏 ​ { x ∼ y } . \displaystyle\mathcal{C}(S)=\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\},\quad\mathbf{C}:=\mathbf{1}\{x\sim y\}\,. (71) Note that by centering embeddings such that 𝟏 ⊤ ​ 𝐄 = 𝟎 \mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{0} one can eliminate 𝟏𝟏 ⊤ \mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top} . The interpretation is very clear: For each token, a (bi-)linear map can learn only one of two possible things (and superpositions thereof): auto-association of a token with itself (i.e. 𝐈 \mathbf{I} ) or hetero-association of a token with its partner (i.e. 𝐂 \mathbf{C} ). Positional Embeddings. Token positions are encoded via sinusoidal embeddings as in [ 29 ] . To model the fact that relevant blocks may appear at arbitrary positions, we add random offsets for each block. Let ω i ∈ ℝ + \omega_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+} , i ∈ [ k ] i\in[k] , denote the embedding frequencies. For a token at relative position t t inside block b b , with offset ϕ b \phi_{b} , we define p b , t = ( sin ⁡ ( ω 1 ​ ( t + ϕ b ) ) , cos ⁡ ( ω 1 ​ ( t + ϕ b ) ) , … , sin ⁡ ( ω k ​ ( t + ϕ b ) ) , cos ⁡ ( ω k ​ ( t + ϕ b ) ) ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ 2 ​ k

. \displaystyle p_{b,t}=\bigl(\sin(\omega_{1}(t+\phi_{b})),\cos(\omega_{1}(t+\phi_{b})),\dots,\sin(\omega_{k}(t+\phi_{b})),\cos(\omega_{k}(t+\phi_{b}))\bigr)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2k}. In the spirit of the previous paragraph, we require that proper induction does not learn or memorize absolute token positions, but only relative ones. We thus require invariance under relative position shifts. It is a key property of sinusoidal embeddings that shifts in position correspond to rotations within each subspace V j V_{j} . More abstractly, the above positional embeddings admit a decomposition into orthogonal two-dimensional subspaces corresponding to different frequencies, i.e. ℝ 2 ​ k = ⨁ j V j \mathbb{R}^{2k}=\bigoplus_{j}V_{j} . The corresponding subgroup P ⊆ O ​ ( d ) P\subseteq O(d) consists of block-diagonal orthogonal matrices of the form 𝐔 = diag ⁡ ( 𝐔 1 , … , 𝐔 k ) , 𝐔 j ∈ S ​ O ​ ( 2 ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{U}=\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{U}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{U}_{k}),\quad\mathbf{U}_{j}\in SO(2). (72) As before, symmetry of the data distribution implies that admissible operators must commute with all 𝐔 ∈ P \mathbf{U}\in P , which already constrains them substantially. Commutation alone, however, does not fully determine the structure used in the main text: additional properties of the sinusoidal embeddings, unrelated to this symmetry, further reduce the admissible operators to the desired polynomial structure in the positional shift operator (see section ˜ A.3 ). Appendix D

In-Context Learning vs. In-Weights Learning D.1 Training details We train a 2-layer, 1-head-per-layer attention-only transformer of width 128 128 on an in-context bigram prediction task with a vocabulary of 16 16 common tokens and 64 64 rare tokens, plus sinusoidal positional embeddings with 4 frequencies (8 features). Each sequence consists of 4 4 bigram demonstrations followed by a query, giving a context length of 9 9 . Tokens within each sequence are sampled as rare with probability 0.9 0.9 , with single-shot demonstrations within context and zero per-sequence frequency variation. To facilitate interpretability, we train the simplified architecture described in section ˜ A.3.2 , with merged key-query matrix, fixed output-value projections, and orthogonal representations. The width of 128 128 is chosen so that the model exactly admits the geometric construction of the bigram circuit on the manifold (base dimension 32 32 , branching ( 1 + H ) L = 4 (1+H)^{L}=4 , hence 4 ⋅ 32 = 128 4\cdot 32=128 ). We optimize with vanilla SGD at learning rate 0.1 0.1 and batch size 256 256 for 250 250 steps; the random seed is fixed at 0 . Every 10 10 steps we estimate top-1 accuracy on two probe distributions, each averaged over 8 8 fresh batches: an ICL probe in which every token is rare, so that the query bigram can only be solved by attending to its in-context demonstration, and an IWL probe in which every token is common and the context is shuffled so the target pair is absent, forcin

g reliance on the in-weights bigram statistics. For the constrained training, we use the same optimization hyperparameters, while ablating all model parameters except for α \alpha , β \beta , γ \gamma , and δ \delta after every step of gradient descent. Compute resources. The model has approximately 0.05 0.05\, M learnable parameters; one full training run (250 SGD steps at batch size 256, plus the periodic ICL and IWL probes) takes well under a minute on a single consumer-grade GPU and uses less than 100 100\, MB of GPU memory. Constrained training adds a single per-step IMIR projection, which is dominated by a constant-size matrix multiply and does not change the wall-clock cost in any noticeable way. D.2 Circuit Ablations In this appendix, we causally test the circuit interpretation given in section ˜ 4.2 . We take the model trained as described in section ˜ D.1 and ablate all of its parameters except for a chosen subset of the four highlighted IMIR directions; we then re-evaluate the training loss and the ICL and IWL probe accuracies (defined in section ˜ D.1 ). Each row of table ˜ 1 corresponds to one such subset. The results confirm our interpretation: keeping only ( α , β , γ ) (\alpha,\beta,\gamma) preserves ICL accuracy ( 0.75 0.75 , vs. 0.77 0.77 for the unablated model) while destroying IWL; keeping only δ \delta yields near-perfect IWL accuracy and no ICL; and removing any single direction from ( α , β , γ ) (\alpha,\beta,\gamma) destroys ICL entirely, confirming

that these three directions jointly implement the induction head. Table 1: Ablation study confirming that the α ​ β ​ γ \alpha\beta\gamma -circuit implements In-Context Learning (ICL), while the δ \delta -circuit accounts for In-Weights Learning (IWL). Ablated except Train Loss ICL Accuracy IWL Accuracy all (original) 1.41 0.77 0.22 α , β , γ , δ \alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta 1.06 0.76 0.14 α , β , γ \alpha,\beta,\gamma 1.03 0.75 0.10 δ \delta 4.34 0.01 0.99 α , β \alpha,\beta 4.38 0.00 0.06 α , γ \alpha,\gamma 3.99 0.05 0.00 β , γ \beta,\gamma 7.47 0.03 0.00 D.3 All circuits during training As described in section ˜ 4.2 , the IMIR of our two-layer, single-head model is 28-dimensional. We additively decompose the model’s key–query and output weights into these 28 basis directions, and we track the resulting coefficients throughout training. fig. ˜ 6 plots every coefficient, across the data regimes considered in section ˜ 4.2 . Only the four highlighted directions ( α \alpha , β \beta , γ \gamma , and δ \delta ) grow appreciably during training; this is the evidence behind our claim in section ˜ 4.2 that learning concentrates on these four directions. Only the four highlighted directions ( α \alpha , β \beta , γ \gamma

, δ \delta ) grow appreciably during training. (A2) Geometry. Token embeddings are orthonormal (the associative-memory idealization; lex-invariant rare embeddings share the common tokens’ norm and are pairwise orthogonal up to O ​ ( | 𝒱 | − 1 ) O(|{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{V}}}|^{-1}) fluctuations), under the standing assumptions of section ˜ A.3.2 , and all gradients are population (data-averaged) gradients, consistent with Corollary ˜ 1 . The ICL gradient is therefore proportional to b {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}} in this limit. □ \square Appendix E Competing Induction Heads This appendix supports section ˜ 4.3 , which studies the dependence of the winning ICL circuit on initialization in a 3-layer transformer. E.1 Training details We train a 3-layer, 1-head-per-layer attention-only transformer of width 256 256 on the in-context bigram task of section ˜ D.1 restricted to the all-rare regime ( f r = 1.0 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{r}}}=1.0 ), so the only solution to the task is an in-context induction circuit. The vocabulary is 16 16 common and 16 16 rare tokens, sinusoidal positional embeddings with 4 frequencies (

8 features), and each sequence consists of 4 4 bigram demonstrations followed by a query (context length 9 9 ); both burstiness and within-class variability are set to their neutral values ( b = 1 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}b}}=1 , f v = 0 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{v}}}=0 ). We train the simplified architecture of section ˜ A.3.2 , with merged key-query matrices, fixed output-value projections, and orthogonal representations. The width 256 = 32 × ( 1 + H ) L 256=32\times(1+H)^{L} with H = 1 H=1 and L = 3 L=3 is chosen so that the model exactly admits the geometric construction of all three competing induction circuits on the manifold (base dimension 32 32 , branching ( 1 + H ) L = 8 (1+H)^{L}=8 ). Optimization uses vanilla SGD at learning rate 0.1 0.1 , micro-batch size 64 64 , gradient-accumulation factor 2 2 (effective batch 128 128 ), for at most 12 , 000 12{,}000 steps, with early-stop once the training cross-entropy drops below 0.5 0.5 . We constrain the dynamics to the IMIR by projecting 𝐖 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}} onto the seven-dimensional sub-manifold spanned by the named circuit units α , α ′ , β , β ′ , β ′′ , γ , γ ′ \alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\p

rime\prime},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime} after every SGD step. For the phase-transition scan of fig. ˜ 4 , each seed samples ( s β , s β ′′ ) ∼ Uniform ​ ( 0 , 0.6 ) 2 (s_{\beta},s_{\beta^{\prime\prime}})\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(0,\,0.6)^{\!2} i.i.d., zeroes every other IMIR direction, and sets the initial strengths of β \beta and β ′′ \beta^{\prime\prime} to s β s_{\beta} and s β ′′ s_{\beta^{\prime\prime}} respectively; the seed is then plotted at ( s β , s β ′′ ) (s_{\beta},s_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}) and colored by the winning circuit. The winning circuit is identified post-training by causal ablation on a fixed evaluation batch: we rebuild three reduced transformers from the trained IMIR coordinates, retaining only one of { α , β , γ } \{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\} , { α , β ′ , γ ′ } \{\alpha,\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\} , or { α ′ , β ′′ , γ ′ } \{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\} at a time, and pick the one with the lowest cross-entropy at the query position. The same fixed batch is used across all seeds so the three ablation losses are directly comparable. Compute resources. Each individual seed trains a 3-layer transformer of width 256 256 ( ∼ 0.2 {\sim}0.2\, M learnable parameters) for at most 12 , 000 12{,}000 SGD steps with effective batch size 128 128 , taking a few minutes on a single GPU and using under 0.5 0.5\, GB of GPU memory. The phase-transition scan parallelizes trivially across seeds: we run it on an 8-GPU node via per-seed sharding, with one pe

rsistent worker process per GPU pulling seeds from a shared queue. The scan reported in fig. ˜ 4 aggregates several thousand seeds and completes in a few hours of wall-clock on the 8-GPU node. E.2 Training Dynamics Figure 7: Training dynamics of 12 random seeds of a 3-layer, single-head Transformer trained on in-context bigrams (cf. section ˜ 4.3 ). For each seed, the top panel shows the training cross-entropy and the bottom panel the strengths of the seven named circuit units ( α \alpha , α ′ \alpha^{\prime} , β \beta , β ′ \beta^{\prime} , β ′′ \beta^{\prime\prime} , γ \gamma , γ ′ \gamma^{\prime} ); the units that grow together indicate which of the three competing induction circuits wins. Appendix F Towards Automated Interpretability F.1 Training Details We train a 5-layer, 1-head-per-layer attention-only transformer of width 1024 1024 on the k k -hop induction task with k = 2 k=2 hops, vocabulary of 16 16 common and 32 32 rare tokens, sinusoidal positional embeddings with 4 frequencies (8 features), and 9 context blocks per sequence. The all-rare regime f r = 1.0 {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}f_{r}}}=1.0 is used; an implicit-curriculum loss samples the target uniformly from the k k hop endpoints { t 1 , … , t k } \{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\} along the query chain, and within-chain blocks are emitted in reverse-hop order so that the target-bearing hop sits furthest from the query position

. The width 1024 = 32 × ( 1 + H ) L 1024=32\times(1+H)^{L} with H = 1 H=1 and L = 5 L=5 admits the orthogonal-subspace construction of all IMIR directions exactly. Optimization uses AdamW at learning rate 10 − 3 10^{-3} , micro-batch size 1 1 , gradient-accumulation factor 32 32 (effective batch 32 32 ), for 50 , 000 50{,}000 optimizer steps; the random seed is fixed at 6 6 . Compute resources. The model has approximately 32 32\, M learnable parameters; the bulk of the cost comes from the layer-wise attention matrices over the relatively long context. We launch training via torchrun on a single 8-GPU node, with the model wrapped in DistributedDataParallel and gradients all-reduced on the final micro-batch of each accumulation window. One full training run (50,000 optimizer steps, effective batch 32 ⋅ 8 = 256 32\cdot 8=256 across ranks) takes a few hours of wall-clock on this hardware. The circuit-detection routine of section ˜ F.2 runs post-training on a single GPU and completes in minutes: each greedy round evaluates one forward pass per remaining IMIR direction on a fixed eval batch, so the total cost is quadratic in the number of non-zero IMIR directions of the trained model. F.2 Circuit Detection Algorithm Our circuit-detection routine performs greedy backward elimination on the manifold coordinates of the trained model. Starting from the full set of IMIR directions, it repeatedly drops the single direction whose ablation increases the loss the least, and stops when even

the cheapest available ablation would push the loss on a fixed evaluation batch above a user-supplied budget τ \tau . The surviving directions, sorted by their per-circuit ablation cost, are the algorithm’s identification of the model’s essential circuits. {i}};\,\mathcal{B}\big) ⊳ \triangleright tentative ablation of direction i i 12: if ℒ i < ℒ ⋆ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{i}<{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\star} then 13: ℒ ⋆ ← ℒ i {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\star}\leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{i} ; i ⋆ ← i i_{\star}\leftarrow i 14: end if 15: if ℒ ⋆ ≤ ρ ⋅ ℒ cur {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\star}\leq\rho\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\mathrm{cur}} then 16: break ⊳ \triangleright free ablation found; skip rest of scan 17: end if 18: end for 19: if ℒ ⋆ ≥ τ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\star}\geq\tau then 20: break ⊳ \triangleright even the cheapest ablation crosses the budget 21: end if 22: 𝐖 ← 𝐖 − s i ⋆ ​ 𝐞 i ⋆ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\

pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}}\leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}}-s_{i_{\star}}\,\mathbf{e}_{i_{\star}} ⊳ \triangleright commit the ablation 23: 𝒜 ← 𝒜 ∖ { i ⋆ } \mathcal{A}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}\setminus\{i_{\star}\} ; ℒ cur ← ℒ ⋆ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\mathrm{cur}}\leftarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\star} 24: end while 25: 26: return 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , sorted by per-circuit ablation cost ℒ ​ ( f 𝐖 − s i ​ 𝐞 i ; ℬ ) − ℒ cur {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}(f_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathbf{W}}}-s_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}};\,\mathcal{B})-{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}\mathcal{L}}}_{\mathrm{cur}} in descending order. The free-ablation slack ρ \rho is a small optimization: once the inner scan finds an ablation whose cost is within a factor ρ \rho of the current loss, we accept it without finishing the round. This keeps each round at O ​ ( | 𝒜

| ) O(|\mathcal{A}|) in the worst case but typically much cheaper when several directions are obviously prunable. The randomized scan order avoids the bias of always probing the same prefix first. The fixed evaluation batch ℬ \mathcal{B} and the deterministic RNG seed used by the embedding layer ensure that two evaluations of the same model produce identical losses, which is required for the greedy comparisons to be meaningful. We use the values τ = 100 \tau=100 and ρ = 1.01 \rho=1.01 in our experiments. F.3 Discovered Circuits We run the greedy backward-elimination procedure of Algorithm ˜ 1 on two trained 5-layer transformers solving the k = 2 k=2 hop induction task, and report the discovered circuits in tables ˜ 2 and 3 . In addition to the four units one would expect from a canonical induction-head construction — a previous-token head, two item-match heads chained through value rotations, and a readout that inverts the chain — the algorithm consistently recovers a small number of aiding units that further lift accuracy without being part of any standard induction template. We separate the two groups in the tables for clarity. The aiding units are interesting on their own: removing them drops the isolated subcircuit’s accuracy from ∼ 0.92 \sim 0.92 down to ∼ 0.6 \sim 0.6 ( table ˜ 2 , 0.922 → 0.594 0.922\to 0.594 ), which is much more than a slight refinement. They appear to compose with the canonical induction units to form a slightly redundant ensemble that exploits mult

iple paths through the residual stream simultaneously. This kind of structure would be invisible to existing automated circuit discovery algorithms with granularity at the attention head level. We list the four canonical units under Essential and the recovered extras under Aiding in each circuit’s table.
